-Calculus.

8.2.
Context Typing, Synthesis and Erasure

In this section, we establish properties about contexts used for contextual equivalence in
\lambda^{*}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{189617}{197518}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\mathsf{G}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{189617}{197518}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\mathsf{G}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{132731}{138262}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\mathsf{G}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{94808}{98759}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{G}$}}}}}}
. We essentially lift
the dependency synthesis (
Figure

9
) to a notion of dependency synthesis for
\lambda^{*}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{248874}{197518}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\mathsf{M}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{248874}{197518}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\mathsf{M}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{174211}{138262}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\mathsf{M}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{124437}{98759}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{M}$}}}}}}

contexts (
Figure

16
).

Definition 8.1 (Dependency Erasure).

We write
\ulcorner g\urcorner
(
\ulcorner b\urcorner
), for the erasure of effect dependencies from
\lambda^{*}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{189617}{197518}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\mathsf{G}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{189617}{197518}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\mathsf{G}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{132731}{138262}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\mathsf{G}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{94808}{98759}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{G}$}}}}}}
graph terms (bindings), yielding
their unannotated version in
\lambda^{*}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{248874}{197518}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\mathsf{M}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{248874}{197518}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\mathsf{M}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{174211}{138262}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\mathsf{M}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{124437}{98759}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{M}$}}}}}}
(
Section

4
), as well as
\ulcorner C\urcorner
for erasing dependencies in

\lambda^{*}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{189617}{197518}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\mathsf{G}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{189617}{197518}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\mathsf{G}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{132731}{138262}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\mathsf{G}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{94808}{98759}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{G}$}}}}}}
contexts from
Figure

17
into
\lambda^{*}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{248874}{197518}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\mathsf{M}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{248874}{197518}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\mathsf{M}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{174211}{138262}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\mathsf{M}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{124437}{98759}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{M}$}}}}}}
contexts from
Figure

16
.

Definition 8.2 (Context Dependency Synthesis).

We write

{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\Delta}\,\vdash\,C:(\Gamma^{\prime\,\varphi^{\prime}};T^{\prime\,q^{\prime}}\ {\color[rgb]{0.6602,0.1484,0.3086}\bm{\varepsilon^{\prime}}})\Rrightarrow(\Gamma^{\,\varphi};T^{\,q}\ {\color[rgb]{0.6602,0.1484,0.3086}\bm{\varepsilon}}){\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\ \leadsto\mathbf{C}\mathbin{\bullet}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\Delta}^{\prime}}

for context synthesis, obtained from lifting the dependency synthesis (
Figure

9
) to context typing derivations
(
Figure

16
).

Intuitively, the synthesis over contexts yields the annotated context with respect to the ambient last-uses coeffect
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\Delta}
,
with
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\Delta^{\prime}}
being the ambient last uses at the hole of the context. The functional properties of dependency synthesis
on
\lambda^{*}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{248874}{197518}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\mathsf{M}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{248874}{197518}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\mathsf{M}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{174211}{138262}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\mathsf{M}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{124437}{98759}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{M}$}}}}}}
terms (
Section

6.4
) carry over analogously to contexts:

Lemma 8.3 (Soundness of Context Dependency Synthesis).

If

{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\Delta}\,\vdash\,C:(\Gamma^{\prime\,\varphi^{\prime}};T^{\prime\,q^{\prime}}\ {\color[rgb]{0.6602,0.1484,0.3086}\bm{\varepsilon^{\prime}}})\Rrightarrow(\Gamma^{\,\varphi};T^{\,q}\ {\color[rgb]{0.6602,0.1484,0.3086}\bm{\varepsilon}}){\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\ \leadsto\mathbf{C}\mathbin{\bullet}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\Delta}^{\prime}}

then

\mathbf{C}:(\Gamma^{\prime\,\varphi^{\prime}}\mathbin{\bullet}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\Delta^{\prime}};T^{\prime\,q^{\prime}}\ {\color[rgb]{0.6602,0.1484,0.3086}\bm{\varepsilon^{\prime}}})\Rrightarrow(\Gamma^{\,\varphi}\mathbin{\bullet}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\Delta};T^{\,q}\ {\color[rgb]{0.6602,0.1484,0.3086}\bm{\varepsilon}})

Proof.

By induction over the derivation
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\Delta}\,\vdash\,C:(\Gamma^{\prime\,\varphi^{\prime}};T^{\prime\,q^{\prime}}\ {\color[rgb]{0.6602,0.1484,0.3086}\bm{\varepsilon^{\prime}}})\Rrightarrow(\Gamma^{\,\varphi};T^{\,q}\ {\color[rgb]{0.6602,0.1484,0.3086}\bm{\varepsilon}}){\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\ \leadsto\mathbf{C}\mathbin{\bullet}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\Delta}^{\prime}}
.
∎

Lemma 8.4 (Context Dependency Synthesis is Total).

If

C:(\Gamma^{\prime\,\varphi^{\prime}};T^{\prime\,q^{\prime}}\ {\color[rgb]{0.6602,0.1484,0.3086}\bm{\varepsilon^{\prime}}})\Rrightarrow(\Gamma^{\,\varphi};T^{\,q}\ {\color[rgb]{0.6602,0.1484,0.3086}\bm{\varepsilon}})

then for all
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\Delta}
with
\operatorname{dom}({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\Delta})=\operatorname{dom}(\Gamma)
there is
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\Delta^{\prime}}
with
\operatorname{dom}({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\Delta^{\prime}})=\operatorname{dom}(\Gamma^{\prime})
and
an annotated context
\mathbf{C}
where

{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\Delta}\,\vdash\,C:(\Gamma^{\prime\,\varphi^{\prime}};T^{\prime\,q^{\prime}}\ {\color[rgb]{0.6602,0.1484,0.3086}\bm{\varepsilon^{\prime}}})\Rrightarrow(\Gamma^{\,\varphi};T^{\,q}\ {\color[rgb]{0.6602,0.1484,0.3086}\bm{\varepsilon}}){\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\ \leadsto\mathbf{C}\mathbin{\bullet}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\Delta}^{\prime}}

and
\ulcorner\mathbf{C}\urcorner=C
.

Proof.

By induction over the context typing derivation for
C
.
∎

Lemma 8.5 (Context Re-Synthesis).

If

C:(\Gamma^{\prime\,\varphi^{\prime}}\mathbin{\bullet}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\Delta^{\prime}};T^{\prime\,q^{\prime}}\ {\color[rgb]{0.6602,0.1484,0.3086}\bm{\varepsilon^{\prime}}})\Rrightarrow(\Gamma^{\,\varphi}\mathbin{\bullet}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\Delta};T^{\,q}\ {\color[rgb]{0.6602,0.1484,0.3086}\bm{\varepsilon}})

then

{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\Delta}\,\vdash\,\ulcorner C\urcorner:(\Gamma^{\prime\,\varphi^{\prime}};T^{\prime\,q^{\prime}}\ {\color[rgb]{0.6602,0.1484,0.3086}\bm{\varepsilon^{\prime}}})\Rrightarrow(\Gamma^{\,\varphi};T^{\,q}\ {\color[rgb]{0.6602,0.1484,0.3086}\bm{\varepsilon}}){\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\ \leadsto{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}C}\mathbin{\bullet}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\Delta^{\prime}}}.

Proof.

By induction over the context typing derivation for
C
.
∎

Lemma 8.6 (Decomposition).

If
\Gamma^{\,\varphi}\mathbin{\bullet}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\Delta}\,\vdash\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}C[\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}g}\,]}:T^{\,q}\ {\color[rgb]{0.6602,0.1484,0.3086}\bm{\varepsilon}}
, then

\Gamma^{\prime\,\varphi^{\prime}}\mathbin{\bullet}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\Delta^{\prime}}\,\vdash\,g:S^{\,p}\ {\color[rgb]{0.6602,0.1484,0.3086}\bm{\varepsilon^{\prime}}}
and
C:(\Gamma^{\prime\,\varphi^{\prime}}\mathbin{\bullet}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\Delta^{\prime}};S^{\,p}\ {\color[rgb]{0.6602,0.1484,0.3086}\bm{\varepsilon^{\prime}}})\Rrightarrow(\Gamma^{\,\varphi}\mathbin{\bullet}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\Delta};T^{\,q}\ {\color[rgb]{0.6602,0.1484,0.3086}\bm{\varepsilon}})

for some
\Gamma^{\prime}
,
\varphi^{\prime}
,
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\Delta^{\prime}}
,
S
,
p
, and
{\color[rgb]{0.6602,0.1484,0.3086}\bm{\varepsilon^{\prime}}}
.

Proof.

By induction over the context
C
.
∎

Lemma 8.7 (Plugging).

If
\Gamma^{\prime\,\varphi^{\prime}}\mathbin{\bullet}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\Delta^{\prime}}\,\vdash\,g:S^{\,p}\ {\color[rgb]{0.6602,0.1484,0.3086}\bm{\varepsilon^{\prime}}}
and
C:(\Gamma^{\prime\,\varphi^{\prime}}\mathbin{\bullet}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\Delta^{\prime}};S^{\,p}\ {\color[rgb]{0.6602,0.1484,0.3086}\bm{\varepsilon^{\prime}}})\Rrightarrow(\Gamma^{\,\varphi}\mathbin{\bullet}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\Delta};T^{\,q}\ {\color[rgb]{0.6602,0.1484,0.3086}\bm{\varepsilon}})
,
then
\Gamma^{\,\varphi}\mathbin{\bullet}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\Delta}\,\vdash\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}C[\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}g}\,]}:T^{\,q}\ {\color[rgb]{0.6602,0.1484,0.3086}\bm{\varepsilon}}
.

Proof.

By induction over the context typing for
C
.
∎

Lemma 8.8 (Synthesis Plugging).

If

{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\Delta}\,\vdash\,C:(\Gamma^{\prime\,\varphi^{\prime}};T^{\prime\,q^{\prime}}\ {\color[rgb]{0.6602,0.1484,0.3086}\bm{\varepsilon^{\prime}}})\Rrightarrow(\Gamma^{\,\varphi};T^{\,q}\ {\color[rgb]{0.6602,0.1484,0.3086}\bm{\varepsilon}}){\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\ \leadsto\mathbf{C}\mathbin{\bullet}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\Delta^{\prime}}}

and

\Gamma^{\,\varphi}\mathbin{\bullet}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\Delta^{\prime}}\vdash g:T^{\prime\,q^{\prime}}\ {\color[rgb]{0.6602,0.1484,0.3086}\bm{\varepsilon^{\prime}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\ \leadsto\mathbf{g}\mathbin{\bullet}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\Delta^{\prime}}|_{{\color[rgb]{0.6602,0.1484,0.3086}{\color[rgb]{0.6602,0.1484,0.3086}\bm{\varepsilon^{\prime}}}\mathord{*}}}}

then

\Gamma^{\,\varphi}\mathbin{\bullet}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\Delta}\,\vdash\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}C[\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}g}\,]}:T^{\,q}\ {\color[rgb]{0.6602,0.1484,0.3086}\bm{\varepsilon}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\ \leadsto{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\mathbf{C}[\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\mathbf{g}}}\,]}\mathbin{\bullet}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\Delta}|_{{\color[rgb]{0.6602,0.1484,0.3086}{\color[rgb]{0.6602,0.1484,0.3086}\bm{\varepsilon}}\mathord{*}}}}.

Proof.

By induction over the context
C
.
∎

8.3.
Logical and Contextual Equivalence for
\lambda^{*}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{189617}{197518}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\mathsf{G}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{189617}{197518}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\mathsf{G}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{132731}{138262}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\mathsf{G}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{94808}{98759}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{G}$}}}}}}
with Hard Dependencies

Context for Contextual Equivalence

C::=\square\mid\text{{{let}}}~{x=C\mathbin{\bullet}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\delta}}~\text{{{in}}}~g\mid\text{{{let}}}~{x=(\lambda y.C\mathbin{\bullet}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\delta})\mathbin{\bullet}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\delta}}~\text{{{in}}}~g\mid\text{{{let}}}~{x=b\mathbin{\bullet}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\delta}}~\text{{{in}}}~C

Context Typing Rules

C:(\Gamma^{\,\varphi}\mathbin{\bullet}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\Delta};T^{\,q}\ {\color[rgb]{0.6602,0.1484,0.3086}\bm{\varepsilon}})\Rrightarrow(\Gamma^{\,\varphi}\mathbin{\bullet}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\Delta};T^{\,q}\ {\color[rgb]{0.6602,0.1484,0.3086}\bm{\varepsilon}})

\displaystyle\frac{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\ \Gamma\,\vdash\,S^{\,p}\ {\color[rgb]{0.6602,0.1484,0.3086}\bm{\varepsilon_{1}}}<:T^{\,q}\ {\color[rgb]{0.6602,0.1484,0.3086}\bm{\varepsilon_{2}}}\end{array}}{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\square:(\Gamma^{\,\varphi}\mathbin{\bullet}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\Delta};{S}^{\,p}\ {\color[rgb]{0.6602,0.1484,0.3086}\bm{\varepsilon_{1}}})\Rrightarrow(\Gamma^{\,\varphi}\mathbin{\bullet}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\Delta};{T}^{\,q}\ {\color[rgb]{0.6602,0.1484,0.3086}\bm{\varepsilon_{2}}})\end{array}}

(c-hole)

\displaystyle\frac{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}C:(\Gamma^{\prime\,\varphi^{\prime}}\mathbin{\bullet}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\Delta^{\prime}};{U^{\prime}}^{\,r^{\prime}}\ {\color[rgb]{0.6602,0.1484,0.3086}\bm{\varepsilon_{1}}})\Rrightarrow(\Gamma^{\,\varphi}\mathbin{\bullet}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\Delta};{U}^{\,r}\ {\color[rgb]{0.6602,0.1484,0.3086}\bm{\varepsilon_{2}}})\hskip 9.24994pt(\Gamma,\,x:{U}^{\,r\mathord{*}\cap\varphi\mathord{*}})^{\varphi,x}\mathbin{\bullet}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\Delta},({\color[rgb]{0.6602,0.1484,0.3086}{\color[rgb]{0.6602,0.1484,0.3086}\bm{\varepsilon_{2}}}\mathord{*}},x)\mapsto x}\,\vdash\,g:{T}^{\,p}\ {\color[rgb]{0.6602,0.1484,0.3086}\bm{\varepsilon_{3}}}\\
x\notin\operatorname{fv}({T})\hskip 9.24994pt\theta=[r/x]\hskip 9.24994pt{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\delta}\sqsubseteq{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\Delta}|_{{\color[rgb]{0.6602,0.1484,0.3086}{\color[rgb]{0.6602,0.1484,0.3086}\bm{\varepsilon_{2}}}\mathord{*}}}}\end{array}}{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\text{{{let}}}~x=C\mathbin{\bullet}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\delta}~\text{{{in}}}~g:(\Gamma^{\prime\,\varphi^{\prime}}\mathbin{\bullet}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\Delta^{\prime}};{U^{\prime}}^{\,r^{\prime}}\ {\color[rgb]{0.6602,0.1484,0.3086}\bm{\varepsilon_{1}}})\Rrightarrow(\Gamma^{\,\varphi}\mathbin{\bullet}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\Delta};({T}^{\,p}\ {\color[rgb]{0.6602,0.1484,0.3086}\bm{\varepsilon_{2}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0.6602,0.1484,0.3086}\bm{\rhd}}}{\color[rgb]{0.6602,0.1484,0.3086}\bm{\varepsilon_{3}}})\theta)\end{array}}

(c-let-1)

\displaystyle\frac{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}C:(\Gamma^{\prime\,\varphi^{\prime}}\mathbin{\bullet}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\Delta^{\prime}};{U^{\prime}}^{\,r^{\prime}}\ {\color[rgb]{0.6602,0.1484,0.3086}\bm{\varepsilon_{1}}})\Rrightarrow({(\Gamma,y:S^{\,p})}^{\,q^{\prime\prime},y}\mathbin{\bullet}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\mathchoice{\scalebox{.5}[1]{$\displaystyle\mapsto$}}{\scalebox{.5}[1]{$\textstyle\mapsto$}}{\scalebox{.5}[1]{$\scriptstyle\mapsto$}}{\scalebox{.5}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\mapsto$}}y};{T}^{\,q}\ {\color[rgb]{0.6602,0.1484,0.3086}\bm{\varepsilon_{2}}})\\
(\Gamma,x:((y:{S}^{\,p})\to^{{\color[rgb]{0.6602,0.1484,0.3086}\bm{\varepsilon_{2}}}}{T}^{\,q})^{\,q^{\prime\prime}\mathord{*}\cap\varphi\mathord{*}})^{\varphi,x}\mathbin{\bullet}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\Delta}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5},({\color[rgb]{0.6602,0.1484,0.3086}{\color[rgb]{0.6602,0.1484,0.3086}\bm{\varepsilon_{4}}}\mathord{*}},x\mapsto x)}\,\vdash\,g:{U}^{\,r}\ {\color[rgb]{0.6602,0.1484,0.3086}\bm{\varepsilon_{3}}}\\
x\notin\operatorname{fv}({U})\hskip 9.24994pt\theta=[q^{\prime\prime}/x]\hskip 9.24994ptq^{\prime\prime}\subseteq\varphi\hskip 9.24994pt{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\delta_{1}}\sqsubseteq{\color[rgb]{0.6602,0.1484,0.3086}{\color[rgb]{0.6602,0.1484,0.3086}\bm{\varepsilon_{2}}}\mathord{*}}\mapsto y}\hskip 9.24994pt{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\delta_{2}}\sqsubseteq{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\Delta}|_{{\color[rgb]{0.6602,0.1484,0.3086}{\color[rgb]{0.6602,0.1484,0.3086}\bm{\varepsilon_{4}}}\mathord{*}}}}\end{array}}{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\text{{{let}}}~x=(\lambda y.C\mathbin{\bullet}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\delta_{1}})\mathbin{\bullet}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\delta_{2}}~\text{{{in}}}~g:(\Gamma^{\prime\,\varphi^{\prime}}\mathbin{\bullet}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\Delta^{\prime}};{U^{\prime}}^{\,r^{\prime}}\ {\color[rgb]{0.6602,0.1484,0.3086}\bm{\varepsilon_{1}}})\Rrightarrow(\Gamma^{\,\varphi}\mathbin{\bullet}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\Delta};({U}^{\,r}\ {\color[rgb]{0.6602,0.1484,0.3086}\bm{\varepsilon_{3}}})\theta)\end{array}}

(c-let-
\lambda
)

\displaystyle\frac{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\Gamma^{\,\varphi}\mathbin{\bullet}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\Delta}\,\vdash\,b:{S}^{\,r}\ {\color[rgb]{0.6602,0.1484,0.3086}\bm{\varepsilon_{1}}}\\
C:(\Gamma^{\prime\,\varphi^{\prime}}\mathbin{\bullet}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\Delta^{\prime}};{U^{\prime}}^{\,r^{\prime}}\ {\color[rgb]{0.6602,0.1484,0.3086}\bm{\varepsilon_{2}}})\Rrightarrow({(\Gamma,x:{S}^{\,r\mathord{*}\cap\varphi\mathord{*}})}^{\,\varphi,x}\mathbin{\bullet}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\Delta},({\color[rgb]{0.6602,0.1484,0.3086}{\color[rgb]{0.6602,0.1484,0.3086}\bm{\varepsilon_{1}}}\mathord{*}},x)\mapsto x};{T}^{\,p}\ {\color[rgb]{0.6602,0.1484,0.3086}\bm{\varepsilon_{3}}})\\
x\notin\operatorname{fv}({T})\hskip 9.24994pt\theta=[r/x]\hskip 9.24994pt{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\delta}\sqsubseteq{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\Delta}|_{{\color[rgb]{0.6602,0.1484,0.3086}{\color[rgb]{0.6602,0.1484,0.3086}\bm{\varepsilon_{1}}}\mathord{*}}}}\end{array}}{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\text{{{let}}}~x=b\mathbin{\bullet}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\delta}~\text{{{in}}}~C:(\Gamma^{\prime\,\varphi^{\prime}}\mathbin{\bullet}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\Delta^{\prime}};{U^{\prime}}^{\,r^{\prime}}\ {\color[rgb]{0.6602,0.1484,0.3086}\bm{\varepsilon_{2}}})\Rrightarrow({{\Gamma}}^{\,\varphi}\mathbin{\bullet}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\Delta};({T}^{\,p}\ {\color[rgb]{0.6602,0.1484,0.3086}\bm{\varepsilon_{1}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0.6602,0.1484,0.3086}\bm{\rhd}}}{\color[rgb]{0.6602,0.1484,0.3086}\bm{\varepsilon_{3}}})\theta)\end{array}}

(c-let-2)

Figure 17.
Context typing rules for the
\lambda^{*}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{170654}{177765}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\mathsf{G}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{170654}{177765}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\mathsf{G}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{119458}{124436}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\mathsf{G}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{85327}{88883}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{G}$}}}}}}
graph IR.

The key point is that we can resort to the metatheory of the direct-style type-and-effect system

\lambda^{*}_{\varepsilon}
, because (1) MNF is a sublanguage of the direct-style language
(
Lemmas

4.2
and
4.3
), and (2)
dependencies are entirely determined by assigned effects (
Lemma

6.1
). Furthermore, effect
dependencies have no operational meaning beyond asserting that they respect the observed
call-by-value evaluation order of effects (
Corollary

6.8
). Thus, from those results we can
appeal to the logical relation and contextual equivalence of
\lambda^{*}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{248874}{197518}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\mathsf{M}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{248874}{197518}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\mathsf{M}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{174211}{138262}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\mathsf{M}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{124437}{98759}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{M}$}}}}}}

(
Section

8.1
) by the erasure and re-synthesis of dependencies to derive their counterparts for
\lambda^{*}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{189617}{197518}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\mathsf{G}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{189617}{197518}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\mathsf{G}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{132731}{138262}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\mathsf{G}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{94808}{98759}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{G}$}}}}}}
:

Definition 8.9 (Logical Equivalence for
\lambda^{*}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{189617}{197518}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\mathsf{G}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{189617}{197518}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\mathsf{G}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{132731}{138262}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\mathsf{G}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{94808}{98759}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{G}$}}}}}}
).

\displaystyle\frac{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\Gamma^{\,\varphi}\models\ulcorner g_{1}\urcorner\mathrel{\approx_{\text{log}}}\ulcorner g_{2}\urcorner:T^{\,q}\ {\color[rgb]{0.6602,0.1484,0.3086}\bm{\varepsilon}}\quad\Gamma^{\,\varphi}\mathbin{\bullet}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\Delta}\vdash\ulcorner g_{1}\urcorner:T^{\,q}\ {\color[rgb]{0.6602,0.1484,0.3086}\bm{\varepsilon}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\ \leadsto{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}g_{1}}\mathbin{\bullet}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\Delta}|_{{\color[rgb]{0.6602,0.1484,0.3086}{\color[rgb]{0.6602,0.1484,0.3086}\bm{\varepsilon}}\mathord{*}}}}\quad\Gamma^{\,\varphi}\mathbin{\bullet}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\Delta}\vdash\ulcorner g_{2}\urcorner:T^{\,q}\ {\color[rgb]{0.6602,0.1484,0.3086}\bm{\varepsilon}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\ \leadsto{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}g_{2}}\mathbin{\bullet}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\Delta}|_{{\color[rgb]{0.6602,0.1484,0.3086}{\color[rgb]{0.6602,0.1484,0.3086}\bm{\varepsilon}}\mathord{*}}}}\end{array}}{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\Gamma^{\,\varphi}\mathbin{\bullet}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\Delta}\models g_{1}\mathrel{\approx_{\text{log}}}g_{2}:T^{\,q}\ {\color[rgb]{0.6602,0.1484,0.3086}\bm{\varepsilon}}\end{array}}

Definition 8.10 (Contextual Equivalence for
\lambda^{*}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{189617}{197518}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\mathsf{G}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{189617}{197518}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\mathsf{G}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{132731}{138262}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\mathsf{G}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{94808}{98759}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{G}$}}}}}}
).

\displaystyle\frac{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\Gamma^{\,\varphi}\models\ulcorner g_{1}\urcorner\mathrel{\approx_{\text{ctx}}}\ulcorner g_{2}\urcorner:T^{\,q}\ {\color[rgb]{0.6602,0.1484,0.3086}\bm{\varepsilon}}\quad\Gamma^{\,\varphi}\mathbin{\bullet}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\Delta}\vdash\ulcorner g_{1}\urcorner:T^{\,q}\ {\color[rgb]{0.6602,0.1484,0.3086}\bm{\varepsilon}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\ \leadsto{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}g_{1}}\mathbin{\bullet}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\Delta}|_{{\color[rgb]{0.6602,0.1484,0.3086}{\color[rgb]{0.6602,0.1484,0.3086}\bm{\varepsilon}}\mathord{*}}}}\quad\Gamma^{\,\varphi}\mathbin{\bullet}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\Delta}\vdash\ulcorner g_{2}\urcorner:T^{\,q}\ {\color[rgb]{0.6602,0.1484,0.3086}\bm{\varepsilon}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\ \leadsto{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}g_{2}}\mathbin{\bullet}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\Delta}|_{{\color[rgb]{0.6602,0.1484,0.3086}{\color[rgb]{0.6602,0.1484,0.3086}\bm{\varepsilon}}\mathord{*}}}}\end{array}}{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\Gamma^{\,\varphi}\mathbin{\bullet}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\Delta}\models g_{1}\mathrel{\approx_{\text{ctx}}}g_{2}:T^{\,q}\ {\color[rgb]{0.6602,0.1484,0.3086}\bm{\varepsilon}}\end{array}}

Intuitively, graph terms are logically/contextually equivalent iff their dependency-erased versions in
\lambda^{*}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{248874}{197518}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\mathsf{M}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{248874}{197518}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\mathsf{M}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{174211}{138262}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\mathsf{M}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{124437}{98759}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{M}$}}}}}}
are logically/contextually
equivalent, and we can recover the original terms by re-synthesizing their dependencies. More precisely, they
are in the image of the synthesis function with respect to the last-use coeffect
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\Delta}
and the effect
{\color[rgb]{0.6602,0.1484,0.3086}\bm{\varepsilon}}

(cf. the synthesis invariant
Lemma

6.1
).

8.3.1.
Properties of Logical Relations

Theorem 8.11 (Fundamental Property).

If
\Gamma^{\,\varphi}\mathbin{\bullet}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\Delta}\,\vdash\,g:T^{\,q}\ {\color[rgb]{0.6602,0.1484,0.3086}\bm{\varepsilon}}
, then
\Gamma^{\,\varphi}\mathbin{\bullet}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\Delta}\models g\mathrel{\approx_{\text{log}}}g:T^{\,q}\ {\color[rgb]{0.6602,0.1484,0.3086}\bm{\varepsilon}}
.

Proof.

\Gamma^{\,\varphi}\mathbin{\bullet}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\Delta}\,\vdash\,g:T^{\,q}\ {\color[rgb]{0.6602,0.1484,0.3086}\bm{\varepsilon}}
implies
\Gamma^{\,\varphi}\,\vdash_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{248874}{197518}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\mathsf{M}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{248874}{197518}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\mathsf{M}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{174211}{138262}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\mathsf{M}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{124437}{98759}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{M}$}}}}}}\,\ulcorner g\urcorner:T^{\,q}\ {\color[rgb]{0.6602,0.1484,0.3086}\bm{\varepsilon}}
and
by the fundamental
Theorem

7.40
, it follows that
\Gamma^{\,\varphi}\models\ulcorner g\urcorner\mathrel{\approx_{\text{log}}}\ulcorner g\urcorner:T^{\,q}\ {\color[rgb]{0.6602,0.1484,0.3086}\bm{\varepsilon}}
.
Finally, by
Lemma

6.3
, we have that
\Gamma^{\,\varphi}\mathbin{\bullet}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\Delta}\vdash\ulcorner g\urcorner:T^{\,q}\ {\color[rgb]{0.6602,0.1484,0.3086}\bm{\varepsilon}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\ \leadsto{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}g}\mathbin{\bullet}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\Delta}|_{{\color[rgb]{0.6602,0.1484,0.3086}{\color[rgb]{0.6602,0.1484,0.3086}\bm{\varepsilon}}\mathord{*}}}}
.
∎

Lemma 8.12 (Congruency of Binary Logical Relations).

The binary logical relation is closed under well-typed program contexts,
i.e., if
\Gamma^{\,\varphi}\mathbin{\bullet}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\Delta}\models g_{1}\mathrel{\approx_{\text{log}}}g_{2}:T^{\,p}\ {\color[rgb]{0.6602,0.1484,0.3086}\bm{\varepsilon}}
,
and
C:(\Gamma^{\,\varphi}\mathbin{\bullet}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\Delta};T^{\,p}\ {\color[rgb]{0.6602,0.1484,0.3086}\bm{\varepsilon}})\Rrightarrow(\Gamma^{\prime\,\varphi^{\prime}}\mathbin{\bullet}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\Delta^{\prime}};T^{\prime\,p^{\prime}}\ {\color[rgb]{0.6602,0.1484,0.3086}\bm{\varepsilon}}^{\prime})
, then
\Gamma^{\prime\,\varphi^{\prime}}\mathbin{\bullet}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\Delta^{\prime}}\models C[g_{1}]\mathrel{\approx_{\text{log}}}C[g_{2}]:T^{\prime\,p^{\prime}}\ {\color[rgb]{0.6602,0.1484,0.3086}\bm{\varepsilon}}^{\prime}
.

Proof.

(1)

By definition of logical equivalence for
\lambda^{*}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{189617}{197518}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\mathsf{G}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{189617}{197518}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\mathsf{G}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{132731}{138262}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\mathsf{G}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{94808}{98759}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{G}$}}}}}}
:

(a)

\Gamma^{\,\varphi}\models\ulcorner g_{1}\urcorner\mathrel{\approx_{\text{log}}}\ulcorner g_{2}\urcorner:T^{\,p}\ {\color[rgb]{0.6602,0.1484,0.3086}\bm{\varepsilon}}
.

(b)

\Gamma^{\,\varphi}\mathbin{\bullet}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\Delta}\vdash\ulcorner g_{1}\urcorner:T^{\,q}\ {\color[rgb]{0.6602,0.1484,0.3086}\bm{\varepsilon}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\ \leadsto{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}g_{1}}\mathbin{\bullet}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\Delta}|_{{\color[rgb]{0.6602,0.1484,0.3086}{\color[rgb]{0.6602,0.1484,0.3086}\bm{\varepsilon}}\mathord{*}}}}
.

(c)

\Gamma^{\,\varphi}\mathbin{\bullet}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\Delta}\vdash\ulcorner g_{2}\urcorner:T^{\,q}\ {\color[rgb]{0.6602,0.1484,0.3086}\bm{\varepsilon}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\ \leadsto{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}g_{2}}\mathbin{\bullet}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\Delta}|_{{\color[rgb]{0.6602,0.1484,0.3086}{\color[rgb]{0.6602,0.1484,0.3086}\bm{\varepsilon}}\mathord{*}}}}
.

(2)

By erasure:
\ulcorner C\urcorner:(\Gamma^{\,\varphi};T^{\,p}\ {\color[rgb]{0.6602,0.1484,0.3086}\bm{\varepsilon}})\Rrightarrow(\Gamma^{\prime\,\varphi^{\prime}};T^{\prime\,p^{\prime}}\ {\color[rgb]{0.6602,0.1484,0.3086}\bm{\varepsilon}}^{\prime})
.

(3)

By the congruence
Lemma

8.12
, and (1), (2):
\Gamma^{\,\varphi}\models{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\ulcorner C\urcorner[\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\ulcorner g_{1}\urcorner}\,]}\mathrel{\approx_{\text{log}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\ulcorner C\urcorner[\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\ulcorner g_{2}\urcorner}\,]}:T^{\,p}\ {\color[rgb]{0.6602,0.1484,0.3086}\bm{\varepsilon}}
.

(4)

(3) is equivalent to
\Gamma^{\,\varphi}\models\ulcorner{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}C[\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}g_{2}}\,]}\urcorner\mathrel{\approx_{\text{log}}}\ulcorner{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}C[\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}g_{2}}\,]}\urcorner:T^{\,p}\ {\color[rgb]{0.6602,0.1484,0.3086}\bm{\varepsilon}}
.

(5)

By assumption, (2), and re-synthesis
Lemma

8.5
:

{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\Delta}\,\vdash\,\ulcorner C\urcorner:(\Gamma^{\,\varphi};T^{\,p}\ {\color[rgb]{0.6602,0.1484,0.3086}\bm{\varepsilon}})\Rrightarrow(\Gamma^{\prime\,\varphi^{\prime}};T^{\prime\,p^{\prime}}\ {\color[rgb]{0.6602,0.1484,0.3086}\bm{\varepsilon}}^{\prime}){\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\ \leadsto{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}C}\mathbin{\bullet}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\Delta^{\prime}}}.

(6)

By (1a), (1c), (5), and synthesis plugging
Lemma

8.8
:

(a)

\Gamma^{\,\varphi}\mathbin{\bullet}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\Delta}\vdash\ulcorner{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}C[\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}g_{1}}\,]}\urcorner:T^{\,q}\ {\color[rgb]{0.6602,0.1484,0.3086}\bm{\varepsilon}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\ \leadsto{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}C[\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}g_{1}}\,]}}\mathbin{\bullet}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\Delta}|_{{\color[rgb]{0.6602,0.1484,0.3086}{\color[rgb]{0.6602,0.1484,0.3086}\bm{\varepsilon}}\mathord{*}}}}
.

(b)

\Gamma^{\,\varphi}\mathbin{\bullet}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\Delta}\vdash\ulcorner{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}C[\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}g_{2}}\,]}\urcorner:T^{\,q}\ {\color[rgb]{0.6602,0.1484,0.3086}\bm{\varepsilon}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\ \leadsto{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}C[\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}g_{2}}\,]}}\mathbin{\bullet}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\Delta}|_{{\color[rgb]{0.6602,0.1484,0.3086}{\color[rgb]{0.6602,0.1484,0.3086}\bm{\varepsilon}}\mathord{*}}}}
.

(7)

(4) and (6) prove the goal.

∎

Theorem 8.13 (Soundness of Binary Logical Relations).

The binary logical relation is sound w.r.t. contextually equivalence, i.e.,
if
\Gamma^{\,\varphi}\mathbin{\bullet}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\Delta}\,\vdash\,g_{1}:T^{\,p}\ {\color[rgb]{0.6602,0.1484,0.3086}\bm{\varepsilon}}
and
\Gamma^{\,\varphi}\mathbin{\bullet}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\Delta}\,\vdash\,g_{2}:T^{\,p}\ {\color[rgb]{0.6602,0.1484,0.3086}\bm{\varepsilon}}
, then

\Gamma^{\,\varphi}\mathbin{\bullet}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\Delta}\models g_{1}\mathrel{\approx_{\text{log}}}g_{2}:T^{\,p}\ {\color[rgb]{0.6602,0.1484,0.3086}\bm{\varepsilon}}
implies

\Gamma^{\,\varphi}\mathbin{\bullet}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\Delta}\models g_{1}\mathrel{\approx_{\text{ctx}}}g_{2}:T^{\,p}\ {\color[rgb]{0.6602,0.1484,0.3086}\bm{\varepsilon}}
.

Proof.

(1)

By definition of logical equivalence for
\lambda^{*}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{189617}{197518}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\mathsf{G}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{189617}{197518}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\mathsf{G}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{132731}{138262}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\mathsf{G}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{94808}{98759}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{G}$}}}}}}
:

(a)

\Gamma^{\,\varphi}\models\ulcorner g_{1}\urcorner\mathrel{\approx_{\text{log}}}\ulcorner g_{2}\urcorner:T^{\,p}\ {\color[rgb]{0.6602,0.1484,0.3086}\bm{\varepsilon}}
.

(b)

\Gamma^{\,\varphi}\mathbin{\bullet}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\Delta}\vdash\ulcorner g_{1}\urcorner:T^{\,q}\ {\color[rgb]{0.6602,0.1484,0.3086}\bm{\varepsilon}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\ \leadsto{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}g_{1}}\mathbin{\bullet}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\Delta}|_{{\color[rgb]{0.6602,0.1484,0.3086}{\color[rgb]{0.6602,0.1484,0.3086}\bm{\varepsilon}}\mathord{*}}}}
.

(c)

\Gamma^{\,\varphi}\mathbin{\bullet}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\Delta}\vdash\ulcorner g_{2}\urcorner:T^{\,q}\ {\color[rgb]{0.6602,0.1484,0.3086}\bm{\varepsilon}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\ \leadsto{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}g_{2}}\mathbin{\bullet}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\Delta}|_{{\color[rgb]{0.6602,0.1484,0.3086}{\color[rgb]{0.6602,0.1484,0.3086}\bm{\varepsilon}}\mathord{*}}}}
.

(2)

By (1a), and soundness
Theorem

7.43
:
\Gamma^{\,\varphi}\models\ulcorner g_{1}\urcorner\mathrel{\approx_{\text{ctx}}}\ulcorner g_{2}\urcorner:T^{\,p}\ {\color[rgb]{0.6602,0.1484,0.3086}\bm{\varepsilon}}
.

(3)

The goal follows by (1b), (1c), (2), and the definition of contextual equivalence for
\lambda^{*}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{189617}{197518}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\mathsf{G}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{189617}{197518}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\mathsf{G}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{132731}{138262}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\mathsf{G}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{94808}{98759}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{G}$}}}}}}
.

∎

8.4.
Soundness of the Optimization Rules

Equational rules for the
\lambda^{*}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{189617}{197518}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\mathsf{G}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{189617}{197518}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\mathsf{G}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{132731}{138262}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\mathsf{G}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{94808}{98759}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{G}$}}}}}}

.

From the above results about logical equivalence for the
\lambda^{*}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{189617}{197518}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\mathsf{G}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{189617}{197518}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\mathsf{G}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{132731}{138262}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\mathsf{G}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{94808}{98759}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{G}$}}}}}}
graph IR obtained by a “round-trip
translation” technique, we are now equipped to prove the soundness of the main paper’s optimization
rules based on the results of
Section

7.9
. The optimization rules are the congruence
closure (with respect to
C
contexts in
Figure

17
) of the equations shown in

Figure

18
. Those are obtainable mechanically from their counterparts in
\lambda^{*}_{\varepsilon}

(
Figure

15
) by using the type-and-effect preserving translation into MNF
(
Section

4.3
) followed by dependency synthesis for a given map
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\Delta}
of last
uses (
Figure

9
). That is, the equational rules in
Figure

18
are
the dependency-annotated versions of their counterparts in MNF.

Theorem 8.14 (Compatibility of the Equational Rules).

Each rule in
Figure

18
is compatible with the logical equivalence.

Proof.

Each individual rule (
dce
), (
comm
), (
\lambda
-hoist
), (
\beta
-inlining
),
and (
e-cse
) can be uniformly proved as follows:

(1)

By dependency erasure, and by
Lemma

4.3
, we have
that both graphs are equated by the direct-style version of the respective rule (
Figure

15
).

(2)

By
Lemmas

7.44
,
7.45
,
7.46
,
7.48
and
7.50
, the erased graphs are logically equivalent
in
\lambda^{*}_{\varepsilon}
.

(3)

By totality and soundness of synthesis (
Lemmas

6.3
and
6.2
),
re-synthesizing the dependencies under the given
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\Delta}
and context
\Gamma^{\,\varphi}
of the erased graphs
yields the initial dependency-annotated versions.

(4)

By (2) and (3), both sides are logically equivalent in
\lambda^{*}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{189617}{197518}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\mathsf{G}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{189617}{197518}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\mathsf{G}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{132731}{138262}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\mathsf{G}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{94808}{98759}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{G}$}}}}}}
(
Definition

8.9
).

∎

Corollary 8.15 (Compatibility of the Optimization Rules).

The optimization rules for
\lambda^{*}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{189617}{197518}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\mathsf{G}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{189617}{197518}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\mathsf{G}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{132731}{138262}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\mathsf{G}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{94808}{98759}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{G}$}}}}}}
, i.e., the congruence closure of the rules in
Figure

18
is compatible in logical equivalence.

Proof.

By
Theorem

8.14
and the congruency
Lemma

8.12
.
∎

Corollary 8.16 (Soundness of the Optimization Rules).

The optimization rules for
\lambda^{*}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{189617}{197518}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\mathsf{G}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{189617}{197518}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\mathsf{G}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{132731}{138262}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\mathsf{G}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{94808}{98759}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{G}$}}}}}}
, i.e., the congruence closure of the rules in
Figure

18
describe contextually equivalent graphs.

Proof.

By
Corollary

8.15
and soundness
Theorem

8.13
.
∎

9.
From Graphs Back to Trees

In this section, we discuss efficient algorithms and heuristics for code generation,
which transform the graph informed by
\lambda^{*}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{189617}{197518}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\mathsf{G}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{189617}{197518}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\mathsf{G}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{132731}{138262}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\mathsf{G}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{94808}{98759}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{G}$}}}}}}
into a tree with nested structures.
This section
follows the same structure as in the main paper
(
Bračevac et al. 2023
)
but explains
the algorithms with greater details
:
(1) we first present the basic scheduling algorithm (
Section

9.1
) which
incorporates dead code elimination;
(2) based on that, a lightweight frequency estimation heuristics (
Section

9.2
)
introduces more flexible code motion;
(3) we finally present a compact scheduling algorithm (
Section

9.3
) for
instruction selection and inlining expressions.
The optimizations are justified by the equational theory in
Section

8.4
.

Figure 19.
The pseudocode of the basic scheduling algorithm with two extensions.
Function
scheduleBlock
decides which nodes are scheduled
into the
current
scope and recursively schedules
inner
scopes.
To generate code for a graph
g
,
we make the call
scheduleBlock
(
g
.
nodes
,

\,\varnothing
,

g
.
result
)
.
The extension to eliminate dead code by soft dependencies (cf.
Section

) is marked in

,
and the extension for frequency estimation and code motion (cf.

) in

.

9.1.
Traversal without Redundant Code

In essence, the block scheduling algorithm traverses a hierarchy of
graph-represented blocks and selects unscheduled nodes to move into
tree-represented blocks (and emit code) based on the node’s dependencies.

Figure

19
shows the vanilla block scheduling algorithm,
which operates over graph IR data structures.
We use
Node
to represent a graph node, and
a few auxiliary functions such as
dataDeps
/
hardDeps
to extract
different kinds of dependencies of a
Node
.

At the beginning of scheduling, we have a scope of
Node
s to schedule and a
symbol representing the final result of the top-level block.
Transitively following the dependencies of the final result,
scheduleBlock

partitions the unscheduled nodes into two
groups: (1) nodes that are scheduled in the current block, and
(2) nodes that will be scheduled into other inner blocks.
This process is recursively applied when encountering lambda nodes
in
traverseNode
.
Schedule decisions are made relying on two properties over nodes,

available
and
reachable
.

Nested Scopes

A node is
available
if all its dependent bound variables
have been introduced under the current path.
A node is scheduled in the
current
block if it is both reachable and
available (Line 29); otherwise, it is moved to the
inner
scope (Line 33).
To this end, we need to
transtively
compute the bound variables depended
by nodes, of which the result is reflected by
boundDeps
.
In
Figure

19
,

path
represents the set of the accumulated bound variables (
e.g.
,
introduced by lambdas) up to the current block.
Both
path
and
scope
need maintaining through the recursive calls
to properly handle scopes and nested blocks.

Dead Code Elimination

A node is
reachable
if it can be back-tracked from the current result node
through effect or data dependencies.
Only reachable nodes are considered for scheduling, which naturally eliminates
dead code (cf. rule
dce
,
Figure

18
).
In
Figure

19
,
reachable
is a priority queue
which reflects the property and enforces the topological ordering.
It is populated with data and effect dependencies along the iteration (Line 37).
As an extension, we discern soft dependencies (
Section

6
) and identify data
and hard dependencies as
reachableHard
(Line 36).
This ensures nodes that are only reachable via soft dependencies can be eliminated.

Complexity

Given the total number of nodes
n
and the maximal depth of nested blocks
k
, the
worst-case asymptotic time complexity is bound by
O(kn^{2})
.
This is because the algorithm traverses over the reachable nodes in order
(bound by
n
,
O(n)
each),
and repeats this process for nested scopes.
In practice, the complexity is bound by
O(kn\log n)
given the decreasing
size of nested scopes and the limited degrees of graph nodes.
To exemplify, scheduling of symbolic execution (cf.
(
Bračevac et al. 2023
)
, Section 7.2)
takes 19.3 sec for 548,976 graph nodes, which is rather efficient.

9.2.
Code Motion by Frequency Estimation

Our basic scheduling algorithm eagerly schedules nodes to their outermost
block, following the equational rules
(comm)
and
(
\lambda
-hoist)

in
Figure

18
.
This is a form of code motion for no extra effort and
generally desirable for functions and loops. For instance,

⬇

List
(1,

2,

3,

4,

5).
map
(
x

=>

x

*

factorial
(
N
))

Lifting the expensive
factorial
out of the lambda is beneficial and feasible
since it does not depend on the bound variable
x
. However, this does not
always generate optimal code. Consider a conditional expression that
transforms an array of complex numbers only in the then-branch,

⬇

if

(
cnd
)

compNums
.
map
(
f
)

else

compNums

Since
compNums
.
map
(
f
)
has no dependency on
cnd
, this statement would be
lifted to the outer scope and always executed regardless of the condition,
thus imposing unnecessary runtime overhead when the else-branch is actually taken.

To avoid this situation, we can
estimate
how frequently a node is used
and move less frequent nodes (
i.e.
, cold) into inner scopes.
We assign a number to each node based on its dependents which
represents how relatively often the node is executed at runtime.
The results of functions and loops are assigned 100,
indicating that they and their dependencies can be executed multiple times (definitely hot).
The results of conditional branches are assigned 0.5 (cold),
assuming each branch is taken with equal probability.
All other nodes are assigned 1.0 (normal).
Numbers above are illustrative and context-insensitive.
Alternative metrics are possible, while what we present here is beneficial to
many code patterns.

Figure

19
highlights in
teal
the changes to the basic
scheduling algorithm to use frequency estimation. Given a node ready to schedule
in the current scope (Line 28-31), we use the function
depFreq
to access the frequency
estimation of its dependencies. Only
those with frequencies greater than 0.5 are considered hot-reachable, and thus
can be included in
current
.
Others are classified to be cold, and are scheduled in inner blocks if all
reaching dependencies are cold.
Given a node scheduled in inner blocks (Line 32-35), its dependencies are considered to have
the same level of warmth as the node itself, ensuring consistent code motion
behavior for code with nested scopes.

The proposed heuristic works as expected in that it
(1) lifts computation out of hot constructs such as loops, and
(2) sinks computation into cold constructs such as conditionals.
Regarding nested scopes, the heuristic prioritizes (1) over (2). Suppose there
is a loop in the current scope and a conditional inside that loop. For a
node inside the conditional, the heuristic tends to lift it out to the current
scope, as the result is still used multiple times during the loop.
On the other hand, if there is a loop inside a conditional in the current scope,
a node used in the loop is not lifted to the current scope, but still subject to
lifting up to the conditional scope to optimize the loop.

Our approach is simpler to implement and more efficient compared to more
sophisticated analyses, such as lazy code motion
(
Knoop et al. 1992
)
, partial
redundancy elimination
(
Kennedy et al. 1999
)
, or even whole-program dataflow analyses.
The estimation heuristics associates a constant factor to each node traversed.
Therefore, it does not change the complexity of the basic scheduling algorithm.

9.3.
Instruction Selection with Compact Traversal

The basic scheduling algorithm generates code that binds
every intermediate expression using
let
.
The result, nevertheless, is not only verbose but also suboptimal, without using
the target-specific primitives.
Consider the following tensor computation snippet,

⬇

val

X

=

Matmul
(
A
,

B
);

val

C

=

Add
(
C
,

X
);

C

The unique use of
X
in
Add
enables further transformation into
destination-passing style using generalized matrix multiplication
GEMM
,
which updates
C
in-place by

C\leftarrow\alpha AB+\beta C
.
Thus, we can match the tree structure
Add
(
C
,

Matmul
(
A
,

B
))
and generate
a single operation, eliminating the intermediate multiplication
X
:

⬇

GEMM
(
A
,

B
,

C
,

alpha
=1.0,

beta
=1.0);

C

This is basically a form of
instruction selection
seen in optimizing
compilers. However, the procedure can be non-trivial on computation
graphs where all consumers of a value need accounting for. A proper solution
on graphs like LLVM’s
SelectionDAG

(
Lattner and Adve 2004
)

takes effort to compose and time to execute.

For the
\lambda^{*}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{189617}{197518}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\mathsf{G}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{189617}{197518}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\mathsf{G}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{132731}{138262}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\mathsf{G}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{94808}{98759}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{G}$}}}}}}
graph IR, we perform a simple but highly useful alternative called
compact
traversal
: we first turn the graph nodes into inlined trees whenever possible,
and then use tree matching algorithms (
e.g.
, maximal munch) to select
the best primitive.
Compact traversal must respect the dependencies to preserve the semantics. Consider the
following code that reads the value from cell
x
and then increments the value of
x
:

⬇

if

(
cond
)

{

val

y

=

!
x
;

inc
(
x
);

println
(
y
)

}

Since
inc
(
x
)
depends on node
y
in an effectful way, inlining
y
to
println
breaks the
semantics.

Compact traversal works on each
current
scope determined by the basic scheduling
algorithm (cf.
Figure

19
). Initially, all nodes in the scope are
viewed as individual trees.

Figure

21
shows the updated main function
scheduleBlock

with compact traversal atop other extensions, and

Figure

20
shows accompanying auxiliary definitions.
In the following, we summarize the compact traversal algorithm in
three steps.

(1)

Track Node Usage.

The key idea of compact traversal is that we only consider inlining for nodes
that are locally defined and locally used exactly once, and are not used in
nested scopes.
To this end, we first identify all candidate nodes for inlining by tracking
node usage.
When scheduling code, we keep track of local definitions and their symbolic
names (Line 12).
We also track how many times a locally defined node is used in the current
scope and inner scopes as a proper value (Line 13-15, 20-21), respectively.

(2)

Compute Local Successors.

After recording the node usage information in the first step, we need to
calculate local successors of a node in the current block (Line 28-31).
A node
x
is a local successor of node
y
if
x
and
y
are scheduled in the
same block and
x
depends on
y
.
The algorithm uses a map to store the local successors of a locally defined node.

(3)

Check Inlining.

Lastly, the algorithm runs a backward pass for all nodes that can be inlined.
This pass checks if all successors of a node are emitted after the point
considered for inlining (
Figure

20
, Line 9-12).
If not, we disable its inlining. Otherwise, we inline
this node and check if any other nodes used by this node can be further inlined
(
Figure

20
, Line 15-18).

A Flexible Framework for Optimization Opportunities

In
Section

9

we have illustrated a series of optimizations possible by simple and composable
algorithms through scheduling graph IR back to nested tree representations.
Opportunities are not limited to the ones aforementioned. For instance, the basic
scheduling algorithm can introduce
instruction scheduling
by assigning a proper
priority value to each node reflecting not only dependency but also timing, thus
tweaking the behavior of the traversal. To conclude, graph IR can be an efficient
and flexible system to generate performant code.
